## Step 1: Embed 1, Structured data (JSON-LD)

File: `00-mastt-dwr-schema.html`. Add an HTML Embed at the top of the post body and paste this as it is. (4,781 characters).

```html
<script type="application/ld+json">
{
  "@context": "https://schema.org",
  "@graph": [
    {
      "@type": "Article",
      "headline": "Construction Delays, Weather Days and Risk Registers: 2026 Statistics",
      "description": "What public records from Texas, Florida, New York and Victoria, and 64 published project risk registers, show about weather days, late penalties and slow approvals.",
      "articleSection": "Project Scheduling",
      "publisher": {
        "@type": "Organization",
        "name": "Mastt",
        "url": "https://www.mastt.com",
        "logo": {
          "@type": "ImageObject",
          "url": "https://cdn.prod.website-files.com/607f739c92f9cfa16116b34d/607f978bbd76e475e2229a43_Mastt%20Logo.svg"
        }
      },
      "mainEntityOfPage": "https://www.mastt.com/research/construction-delay-weather-and-risk-statistics"
    },
    {
      "@type": "Dataset",
      "name": "Construction Delays, Weather Days and Risk Registers: 2026 Statistics",
      "description": "Our own figures come from an internal Mastt study of public records. These include: Texas DOT: More than 30,000 contract time and payment records, daily time charges matched to county rain gauge records, and monthly progress reports for two dates. Florida DOT: A state workbook of 431 contracts for 2025-26. Risk registers: 64 published project risk registers. New York: New York City school construction records covering 5,850 change issues and more than 13,000 project phases, and Thruway capital project records. Victoria: State budget documents for three budget years.",
      "creator": {
        "@type": "Organization",
        "name": "Mastt",
        "url": "https://www.mastt.com"
      }
    },
    {
      "@type": "FAQPage",
      "mainEntity": [
        {
          "@type": "Question",
          "name": "How often does Texas DOT excuse rain days when gauges show little rain?",
          "acceptedAnswer": {
            "@type": "Answer",
            "text": "On 47% of the 2,170 weekdays Texas DOT credited for rain, county gauges showed under a tenth of an inch on the day or the day before, and 32% were fully dry. That is a signal rather than proof, because site conditions can lag rain and gauges can miss patchy storms."
          }
        },
        {
          "@type": "Question",
          "name": "Do wetter seasons cause more late penalties on Texas DOT jobs?",
          "acceptedAnswer": {
            "@type": "Answer",
            "text": "No. 12% of working-day contracts were charged late penalties in both the driest and the wettest fifth of seasons. The three middle fifths were higher, at 16% to 18%, so there is no straight-line pattern."
          }
        },
        {
          "@type": "Question",
          "name": "Why does Florida DOT show 12.5% delay on one measure and 48.7% on another?",
          "acceptedAnswer": {
            "@type": "Answer",
            "text": "The 431 contracts in Florida DOT's 2025-26 workbook used 48.7% more days than planned, but 12.5% more once 32,701 weather days and 19,228 holiday days were removed. The adjustment removes 74% of the extra days."
          }
        },
        {
          "@type": "Question",
          "name": "What share of active Texas DOT jobs are past their allowed days?",
          "acceptedAnswer": {
            "@type": "Answer",
            "text": "About one in six. 17.1% of active jobs in January 2017 and 16.4% in November 2024 had used more than all of their allowed days."
          }
        },
        {
          "@type": "Question",
          "name": "How big are Texas DOT late penalties?",
          "acceptedAnswer": {
            "@type": "Answer",
            "text": "The median penalty was $18,000, or 0.7% of contract value, and 22% of 10,041 completed construction contracts were charged one. Total penalties of $109 million compare with $57 billion of contract awards."
          }
        },
        {
          "@type": "Question",
          "name": "Which risks do project risk registers leave out?",
          "acceptedAnswer": {
            "@type": "Answer",
            "text": "Only 14% of 64 public registers list contractor insolvency, 5% treat climate change as a project risk and none mention cyber risk. By contrast 88% list community risks and 83% list approvals."
          }
        },
        {
          "@type": "Question",
          "name": "How long do NYC school change orders take to approve?",
          "acceptedAnswer": {
            "@type": "Answer",
            "text": "The median was 224 days among 1,095 approved change orders in a November 2021 snapshot, and a quarter took longer than 516 days. In Queens the median was 396 days and in Brooklyn 166."
          }
        }
      ]
    }
  ]
}
</script>
```

## Step 2: Text block 1

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

This page is a citable reference on delay, weather and risk in public construction. It draws on Mastt analysis of public records covering:

 - Texas and Florida road contracts.
- New York school and Thruway projects.
- Victorian state projects.
- 64 published project risk registers.

 Every figure is labelled with its period, and outside facts link to their source. Figures for one state or city describe that owner's work only and are not averages for construction as a whole. Findings are signals from records, not proof of cause.

## Step 4: Text block 2

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

## TL;DR

- On **47%** of the 2,170 weekdays Texas DOT credited for rain, county gauges showed under a tenth of an inch on the day or the day before. **32%** were fully dry, against 77% on ordinary charged weekdays.
- Late penalties did not rise with wetter seasons. **12%** of Texas DOT working-day jobs were charged penalties in both the driest and the wettest fifth of seasons, against 16% to 18% in the three fifths between.
- Across 431 contracts in Florida DOT's 2025-26 workbook, days used ran **48.7%** over plan, but **12.5%** over once 32,701 weather days and 19,228 holiday days were removed.
- About **1 in 6** active Texas DOT jobs was past its allowed days, at 17.1% in January 2017 and 16.4% in November 2024. The median late penalty was **$18,000**, or 0.7% of contract value.
- Across 64 public risk registers, **88%** list community risks, **83%** approvals and **75%** weather. Only **14%** list contractor insolvency, **5%** treat climate change as a risk and none mention cyber risk.
- A public hospital bond program in Washington State published the same **17 risks** in all six 2024 reports reviewed. None changed and none closed.
- NYC school change orders took a median **224 days** to approve. Thruway jobs took a median **327 days** from letting to completion, and **47%** of Victorian projects pushed back their finish date.

 
## Do weather days match the rain gauges?

 Texas DOT records each working day on a road contract as charged or credited, with a reason for every credit. Mastt matched 2,170 weekdays credited for rain, wet ground or other precipitation against county rain gauge records for the same dates.

 
### Excused days were wetter, but often not wet

 Gauge readings on the credited weekdays showed:

 - **Almost no rain:** On 47% of those days, gauges showed under a tenth of an inch of rain on the day or the day before.
- **No rain at all:** On 32% they showed none at all.
- **Ordinary charged weekdays:** Over the same period, 85% had under a tenth of an inch.

 That means excused days were clearly wetter than normal days. A credited weekday was far more likely to follow rain than a charged one, with an odds ratio of 6.4.

var set=function(s){ptr=s>=0&&s<filled?s:-1;update();};
grid.addEventListener('pointermove',function(e){if(e.pointerType==='mouse'||e.buttons)set(slotAt(e));});
grid.addEventListener('pointerdown',function(e){if(e.pointerType!=='mouse'&&grid.setPointerCapture)try{grid.setPointerCapture(e.pointerId);}catch(x){}set(slotAt(e));});

var a=ptr>=0?ptr:0,col=Math.floor(a/R),row=a%R,b=0;blocks.forEach(function(x,i){if(a>=x.start&&a<x.start+x.size)b=i;});
var mv={ArrowUp:function(){return col*R+Math.min(R-1,row+1);},ArrowDown:function(){return col*R+Math.max(0,row-1);},ArrowRight:function(){return Math.min(C-1,col+1)*R+row;},ArrowLeft:function(){return Math.max(0,col-1)*R+row;},PageDown:function(){return blocks[Math.min(blocks.length-1,b+1)].start;},PageUp:function(){return a>blocks[b].start?blocks[b].start:blocks[Math.max(0,b-1)].start;},Home:function(){return 0;},End:function(){return filled-1;}}[e.key];

items.forEach(function(b,i){b.addEventListener('click',function(){pin=pin===i?-1:i;update();});
b.addEventListener('pointerenter',function(e){if(e.pointerType==='mouse'){pre=i;update();}});
b.addEventListener('focus',function(){if(vfocus(b)){pre=i;update();}});
b.addEventListener('blur',function(){pre=-1;update();});});}
});

## Step 7: Text block 3

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

### Why the gauge and the entry can differ

 This is a signal, not proof of misuse. Several things can explain a gap between the entry and the gauge:

 - **Patchy storms:** County gauges can miss a patchy storm, and Mastt used the median of the gauges in each county, which can read zero on days when only part of a county gets rain.
- **Lagging site conditions:** Site conditions can also lag rainfall. In Mastt's data, 26% of credited days had a tenth of an inch of rain two days earlier, against 9% of charged days.
- **Published factors:** A paper on adverse-weather analysis for time extensions by [Nguyen and colleagues (Journal of Construction Engineering and Management, 2010)](https://scholarscommons.fgcu.edu/esploro/outputs/journalArticle/Analysis-of-Adverse-Weather-for-Excusable/99383341611206570) lists lingering days and the definition of normal weather among seven factors that cause discrepancies.

 
### Contract wording shapes weather days

 Contract wording shapes how many weather days exist. The [TxDOT contract administration handbook](https://www.txdot.gov/manuals/cst/cah/prosecution_and_progress/contract_time-bceeiiih.html) sets out two points:

 - **Suspension:** Working day charges can be suspended only when work is suspended, and the handbook gives hazardous weather at the engineer's direction as one example.
- **Unsuitable conditions:** It also lists unsuitable weather or soil conditions as a reason work may be suspended while time charges may still apply.

 Weather does not stop the clock on its own. A decision to suspend work or credit a day has to be recorded.

 Other standard forms ask for other evidence:

 - **AIA A201:** The General Conditions list adverse weather as a cause for extending the contract time only when it is documented with data showing conditions were abnormal for the period, under sections 8.3.1 and 15.1.6.2, as [reproduced by Berger Singerman](https://www.bergersingerman.com/news-insights/what-you-need-to-know-about-construction-weather-delay-claims).
- **AS 4000 and AS 4902:** [Holding Redlich](https://holdingredlich.com/rain-rain-go-away-inclement-weather-in-construction-contracts) notes that these give the contractor no weather relief in their standard form.
- **Common amendments:** Projects often amend them to allow an extension of time but not costs, and often require the weather event to be published by the Bureau of Meteorology.

 **What this means for project teams:**

- Attach the nearest gauge reading, or a Bureau of Meteorology record on Australian jobs, to every weather-day entry when it is raised.
- Record the controlling activity affected and the date the entry was made.
- Review credited days against the readings each month, not at completion.
- Treat a gap between the entry and the record as a prompt to ask a question, not as grounds to refuse.

 
## Do wetter seasons mean more late penalties?

 If weather days work as intended, a wet season should stretch a job without costing the contractor. Mastt tested this on 7,178 Texas DOT working-day contracts awarded from 2014 to 2024.

 
### How the test ranked seasons

 Each job was ranked against others awarded in the same year by how much wetter or drier its work window was than normal for the same dates in the same county, then split into fifths:

 - **Wettest fifth:** About 33% more rain days than normal.
- **Driest fifth:** About 32% fewer.

 
### Late penalties did not follow rain

 The result is flat at the extremes:

 - **Share charged:** 12% of working-day jobs in the driest fifth were charged late penalties, and 12% in the wettest fifth.
- **Penalty days:** Average penalty days fell from 3.8 in the driest fifth to 3.0 in the wettest.
- **Middle fifths:** The three middle fifths ran higher, at 16% to 18%, and Mastt could not explain that pattern from these records.

 Rain did not push penalties up in a straight line.

---

## Step 9: Text block 4

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

### Calendar-day contracts behave differently

 Calendar-day contracts, which are almost all maintenance, behave differently because every calendar day counts. Between 1.4% and 2.1% of those jobs were charged penalties in each fifth, with no pattern by wetness.

 In a regression with controls for contract size, award year, district and work type, a wetter work window was linked to:

 - **Longer working-day jobs:** About 3.5% longer against plan for each standard deviation of rain days above normal.
- **Late penalties:** No clear change in the chance of a late penalty.

 
### Limits of the test

 Read these results with three limits in mind:

 - **Measurement error:** Weather is measured at county level, so each job's weather carries error, which would bias these results toward zero.
- **Association only:** They are associations, not causal estimates.
- **Regional shocks:** Hurricanes and other regional shocks can raise both rain and repair work.

 **What this means for project teams:**

- If a contractor blames weather for a late penalty, check first whether weather days were already credited.
- Then compare the job's rain days with the normal for that county and season.

In Texas DOT records, the pattern points to rain credits absorbing most of the rain effect.

 
## How much of Florida DOT's delay is weather and holidays?

 Florida DOT publishes a workbook of completed contracts that records original days, days used, and the weather and holiday days it removes. Mastt analysed the 431 contracts in its 2025-26 workbook. That is one year's cohort of contracts, not all Florida DOT construction.

---

## Step 11: Text block 5

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

### Adjustments remove three quarters of the extra days

 - **Raw days:** These contracts used 213,680 days against 143,722 planned, which is 48.7% over.
- **Adjustments:** Florida DOT removed 32,701 weather days and 19,228 holiday days, leaving 161,751 days used and 12.5% over.
- **Share of extra days:** The two adjustments account for 74% of the 69,958 extra days.

 The adjusted figure still leaves a tail:

 - **After adjustment:** 91 contracts, 21%, ran more than 20% over their time even after the removals.
- **Before adjustment:** 290 contracts, 67%, ran more than 20% over.

var mxZero=function(el){if(!el.hasAttribute('data-final'))el.setAttribute('data-final',el.textContent);var dec=+(el.getAttribute('data-dec')||0);el.textContent=(el.getAttribute('data-pre')||'')+(0).toFixed(dec)+(el.getAttribute('data-suf')||'');};
var mxCount=function(el,g){var to=parseFloat(el.getAttribute('data-to'));if(isNaN(to))return;var fin=el.getAttribute('data-final')||el.textContent,dec=+(el.getAttribute('data-dec')||0),pre=el.getAttribute('data-pre')||'',suf=el.getAttribute('data-suf')||'',f=new Intl.NumberFormat('en-US',{minimumFractionDigits:dec,maximumFractionDigits:dec}),t0=null,d=950;el.setAttribute('data-final',fin);var step=function(t){if(g!==mxGen)return;if(t0===null)t0=t;var p=Math.min(1,(t-t0)/d),e=1-Math.pow(1-p,3);el.textContent=pre+f.format(to*e)+suf;if(p<1)requestAnimationFrame(step);else el.textContent=fin;};requestAnimationFrame(step);};
var mxIn=function(){if(root.classList.contains('mdwrf05-in'))return;mxGen++;var g=mxGen;root.classList.add('mdwrf05-in');root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(function(el){mxCount(el,g);});};
var mxOut=function(){if(!root.classList.contains('mdwrf05-in'))return;mxGen++;root.classList.remove('mdwrf05-in');root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(mxZero);};
if(RM||!('IntersectionObserver' in window)){root.classList.add('mdwrf05-in');}else{root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(mxZero);var io=new IntersectionObserver(function(es){es.forEach(function(e){if(e.isIntersecting&&e.intersectionRatio>=.15){mxIn();}else if(!e.isIntersecting){mxOut();}});},{threshold:[0,.15]});io.observe(root);}
root.querySelectorAll('.mdwrf05-tabs').forEach(function(box){var tabs=[].slice.call(box.querySelectorAll(':scope>.mdwrf05-tablist>.mdwrf05-tab')),panels=[].slice.call(box.querySelectorAll(':scope>.mdwrf05-panel'));var pick=function(v,focus){tabs.forEach(function(t){var on=t.getAttribute('data-v')===v;t.setAttribute('aria-selected',on?'true':'false');t.tabIndex=on?0:-1;if(on&&focus)t.focus();});panels.forEach(function(p){p.hidden=p.getAttribute('data-v')!==v;});box.dispatchEvent(new CustomEvent('mdwrf05-tab',{bubbles:true}));};tabs.forEach(function(t,i){t.addEventListener('click',function(){pick(t.getAttribute('data-v'));});t.addEventListener('keydown',function(e){var k=e.key,j=k==='ArrowRight'?i+1:k==='ArrowLeft'?i-1:-1;if(j<0&&k!=='ArrowLeft')return;e.preventDefault();var n=tabs[(j+tabs.length)%tabs.length];pick(n.getAttribute('data-v'),true);});});});

var set=function(i){if(i===cur)return;cur=i;cols.forEach(function(el,j){el.classList.toggle('mdwrf05-on',j===i);});plot.classList.toggle('mdwrf05-act',i>=0);if(i<0){k.textContent=c.getAttribute('data-avg-label');n.textContent=c.getAttribute('data-avg');w.textContent=c.getAttribute('data-period');}else{var el=cols[i];k.textContent=c.getAttribute('data-val-label');n.textContent=el.getAttribute('data-v');w.textContent=el.getAttribute('data-l');}};

plot.addEventListener('pointerleave',function(){set(-1);});plot.addEventListener('blur',function(){set(-1);});
plot.addEventListener('keydown',function(e){if(e.key==='ArrowRight'||e.key==='ArrowLeft'){e.preventDefault();var i=cur<0?(e.key==='ArrowRight'?0:cols.length-1):cur+(e.key==='ArrowRight'?1:-1);set(Math.max(0,Math.min(cols.length-1,i)));}else if(e.key==='Escape'){set(-1);}});});
}catch(e){}
})();</script>

## Step 14: Text block 6

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

### Counting rules differ by owner

 Florida DOT ties weather relief to the work affected. Its [statewide inspection guide for time extensions (2016-17)](https://www.fdot.gov/docs/default-source/content-docs/construction/constadm/guidelist/inspectguidelist/fy1617/GL-16C.pdf) says extensions for weather, holidays and special events may be granted only when 50% or more of the predetermined controlling work items are affected, or when major repairs to weather damage affect that share. Rules differ by owner and by edition, so check the version your contract references.

 Counting rules differ everywhere. Nguyen and colleagues list these among the causes of discrepancy:

 - The definition of normal weather.
- Weather thresholds.
- Lingering days.
- The difference between work days and calendar days lost.

 The Society of Construction Law's [Delay and Disruption Protocol](https://scl.org.uk/resources/news/second-edition-delay-disruption-protocol), whose second edition appeared in 2017, is the common reference for determining extensions of time and compensation, and it is free to download. An owner reporting 48.7% delay and another reporting 12.5% may be describing the same performance.

 **What this means for project teams:**

- Before comparing delay between owners, check how each one counts weather and holiday days.
- Ask whether the figure is raw or adjusted, and report both when you publish your own.

 
## How often are Texas DOT jobs late, and what do penalties cost?

 About one active Texas DOT job in six is past its allowed time, and 22% of completed jobs are charged late penalties. The penalties are small: the median was $18,000, or 0.7% of contract value.

 
### One in six active jobs is past its allowed days

 A snapshot of active jobs gives a different view from completed contracts. Texas DOT monthly progress reports list active state-let construction contracts with their allowed days and days charged.

 - **January 2017:** 17.1% of 1,519 active jobs had used more than all of their allowed days.
- **November 2024:** The figure was 16.4% of 2,112 jobs.

 About one job in six was past its time on both dates, even though the live book of work grew from $14.5 billion to $40.7 billion in contract value, before any adjustment for inflation.

return dist<=22?best:-1;};

st.addEventListener('pointermove',function(e){if(e.pointerType==='mouse'||e.buttons)set(nearest(e));});

---

## Step 16: Text block 7

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

The spread of time used was steady across both dates:

 - **Under 25% of time used:** About a third of active jobs had used under 25% of their time on both dates (33.9% and 32.2%).
- **75% to 100% used:** About a quarter had used this much (26.2% and 26.9%).

 The late share did not rise as the live book of work nearly tripled in value.

 
### Late penalties are common but small

 Texas DOT contracts set a daily penalty rate, and each penalty equals days late times that rate. Across 10,041 completed construction contracts:

 - **Charged:** 22% were charged late penalties.
- **Median penalty:** The median penalty was 21 days and $18,000, which is 0.7% of contract value.
- **All penalties:** All penalties together came to $109 million on $57 billion of contract awards.

---

## Step 19: Text block 8

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

Contract size changes the picture:

 - **Daily rates:** Daily rates climb from a median $570 on jobs under $100,000 to $2,590 on jobs over $20 million.
- **Penalty size:** Penalties rise in dollars but fall as a share of the contract, from a median of 2.0% on jobs of $100,000 to $500,000 to about 0.4% on jobs over $5 million.
- **Share charged:** The share of jobs charged peaks at about 24% for jobs of $1 million to $20 million.

 **What this means for project teams:** Penalties are common but rarely large.

- Do not count on a median of $18,000 to recover what a late job costs you in extended supervision and disruption.
- Check your daily rate against the real daily cost of delay.
- Treat about one job in six past its time as a rough Texas DOT benchmark, and a far higher share in your own portfolio as a warning sign.

 
## What do public project risk registers cover?

 A risk register is the working list of what could go wrong on a project and what the team will do about it. Mastt analysed the latest public version of 64 project risk registers, 53 Australian and 11 from the United States, published by councils, regulators, state parliaments and transport agencies or released under freedom of information.

 
### Community, approvals and weather lead the list

 The themes that appear most often in the registers are:

 - **Most common:** 88% list community or stakeholder risks, the most common theme.
- **Next most common:** Approvals and permits appear in 83%, weather and flooding in 75% and heritage and culture in 72%.
- **About half:** Cost escalation shows up in 55%, contamination in 52% and ground conditions in 50%.
- **Less often:** Utilities (39%), scope and design change (39%) and supply chain or labour (36%) appear far less often.

## Step 21: Text block 9

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

### Contractor insolvency is rarely listed

 The gaps say as much as the common themes. Only **9 of 64** registers, 14%, list a contractor or subcontractor going insolvent.

 - **Industry context:** ASIC's [Corporate Insolvency Update of October 2026](https://www.asic.gov.au/about-asic/corporate-publications/newsletters/asic-corporate-insolvency-update/asic-corporate-insolvency-update-issue-41-october-2026) lists construction as the most common industry for appointments in 2025-26, at 24.5%, in a year when 14,153 companies entered external administration for the first time.
- **Recent registers:** Only 3 of the 18 Australian registers dated 2022 or later list the risk, and 7 of those 18 come from one network company.
- **Usual fix:** Where a fix is given, it is usually a financial check of the contractor at tender.

 
### Climate change and cyber risk are largely missing

 - **Climate change:** It appears in 6 of the 64 registers but as a risk to the project in only 3, or 5%. The other three mention it in a definitions legend or as the project's own emissions.
- **Cyber risk:** None of the 64 mention it.
- **COVID or pandemics:** 1 in 5 still mention them.

var groups=[].slice.call(grid.querySelectorAll('.mdwrf10-g')),cells=[].slice.call(grid.querySelectorAll('i')),items=[].slice.call(box.querySelectorAll('.mdwrf10-wf-item')),live=box.querySelector('.mdwrf10-wf-live'),tip=mxTip(box),tipEl=box.querySelector(':scope>.mdwrf10-tip');
var owner=[],blocks=[],at=0;W.n.forEach(function(k,i){if(k>0)blocks.push({start:at,size:k});for(var j=0;j<k;j++)owner.push(i);at+=k;});
var filled=owner.length,ptr=-1,pre=-1,pin=W.p,last=-1;
if(!RM){cells.forEach(function(el,s){el.style.setProperty('--d',(s/N*.32).toFixed(3)+'s');});}

var tw=tipEl.offsetWidth,th=tipEl.offsetHeight,cx=cr.left-hb.left+cr.width/2,cy=cr.top-hb.top;
tipEl.style.left=clamp(cx,tw/2,Math.max(tw/2,hb.width-tw/2))+'px';tipEl.style.top=(cy-th-10<0?cr.bottom-hb.top+th+20:cy)+'px';};
var update=function(){var k=ptr>=0?owner[ptr]:pre>=0?pre:pin;
grid.classList.toggle('mdwrf10-act',k>=0);
groups.forEach(function(g,i){g.classList.toggle('mdwrf10-on',i===k);});
items.forEach(function(b,i){b.classList.toggle('mdwrf10-on',i===k);b.classList.toggle('mdwrf10-dim',k>=0&&i!==k);b.setAttribute('aria-pressed',String(pin===i));});

var set=function(s){ptr=s>=0&&s<filled?s:-1;update();};
grid.addEventListener('pointermove',function(e){if(e.pointerType==='mouse'||e.buttons)set(slotAt(e));});
grid.addEventListener('pointerdown',function(e){if(e.pointerType!=='mouse'&&grid.setPointerCapture)try{grid.setPointerCapture(e.pointerId);}catch(x){}set(slotAt(e));});
grid.addEventListener('pointerup',function(e){if(e.pointerType!=='mouse')set(-1);});

grid.addEventListener('pointerleave',function(e){if(e.pointerType==='mouse')set(-1);});
grid.addEventListener('blur',function(){set(-1);});
grid.addEventListener('focus',function(){if(vfocus(grid)&&ptr<0)set(0);});
grid.addEventListener('keydown',function(e){if(e.key==='Escape'&&ptr>=0){e.preventDefault();set(-1);return;}
var a=ptr>=0?ptr:0,col=Math.floor(a/R),row=a%R,b=0;blocks.forEach(function(x,i){if(a>=x.start&&a<x.start+x.size)b=i;});
var mv={ArrowUp:function(){return col*R+Math.min(R-1,row+1);},ArrowDown:function(){return col*R+Math.max(0,row-1);},ArrowRight:function(){return Math.min(C-1,col+1)*R+row;},ArrowLeft:function(){return Math.max(0,col-1)*R+row;},PageDown:function(){return blocks[Math.min(blocks.length-1,b+1)].start;},PageUp:function(){return a>blocks[b].start?blocks[b].start:blocks[Math.max(0,b-1)].start;},Home:function(){return 0;},End:function(){return filled-1;}}[e.key];
if(!mv)return;e.preventDefault();set(clamp(ptr>=0?mv():0,0,Math.max(0,filled-1)));});
var ul=box.querySelector('.mdwrf10-wf-legend');
if(ul){ul.addEventListener('pointerleave',function(){if(pre>=0){pre=-1;update();}});
items.forEach(function(b,i){b.addEventListener('click',function(){pin=pin===i?-1:i;update();});
b.addEventListener('pointerenter',function(e){if(e.pointerType==='mouse'){pre=i;update();}});
b.addEventListener('focus',function(){if(vfocus(b)){pre=i;update();}});
b.addEventListener('blur',function(){pre=-1;update();});});}
});
})();
}catch(e){}
})();</script>

## Step 24: Text block 10

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

**What this means for project teams:**

- Use the top themes as a checklist, and ask why if your register skips community, approvals or weather.
- Add contractor insolvency with a trigger to recheck the contractor's finances during the job, not only at tender.
- For long-lived assets, put climate risk in the project register where the team will see it.

 
## What does an unchanged risk register look like?

 It looks like the same table copied from one report to the next. In six 2024 reports from one public hospital program, the same 17 risks appeared word for word and no risk was ever closed.

 
### The same 17 risks appear all year

 One public hospital bond program in Washington State publishes its risk table inside each monthly status report. Mastt compared the table across the six 2024 reports it holds, for January, March, April, May, November and December. The comparison found:

 - **Identical text:** The risk text is identical in all six.
- **Same 17 risks:** The same 17 risks appear word for word, typos included.
- **Reused numbers:** Two risk numbers are used twice.
- **No closures:** No risk is marked closed at any point.

---

## Step 26: Text block 11

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

### What a monitored register looks like

 The 2025 tables look different:

 - **June 2025:** The table gains a closed and realised section.
- **September 2025:** It carries a rating and a response strategy for each risk.
- **November and December 2025:** It is a dated register with owners, ratings, strategies, mitigation actions and a list of closed and realised risks.

 The rebuild shows the program moved to a structure that records change. It does not show how risks were managed in 2024 outside the published table.

 The [ISO 31000:2018](https://committee.iso.org/standard/65694.html) guidelines describe a process of identifying, analysing, evaluating, treating, monitoring and communicating risks, and say any organisation, whatever its size, activity or sector, can use them. A published table that shows no change from January to December gives no sign that monitoring took place.

 **What this means for project teams:**

- Date every review and show what moved since the last one.
- Record changes in rating, owner and status, and close risks explicitly when they pass or occur.
- Publish the closed list next to the open one.

 
## How long do approvals and delivery take?

 Approvals can take months, and finish dates often move. A New York City school change took a median 224 days to approve, Thruway contracts took a median 327 days from letting to completion, and 47% of Victorian projects in two or more budgets had their finish date pushed back.

 
### NYC school change orders

 NYC School Construction Authority records show how long a change takes to approve. Of 5,850 change issues in a November 2021 snapshot, 1,095 had reached an approved change order.

 - **Median:** The median time from a change being raised to its approval was 224 days.
- **Slow tail:** A quarter took longer than 516 days, and one in ten took close to 1,000 days.
- **Queens:** The median was 396 days.
- **Brooklyn:** The median was 166 days.

Axis stops at 1,500 days, and 4% of approvals took longer. Five approvals coded to an unknown borough are not shown.</p></div>
<script>(function(){var root=document.getElementById('mastt-dwr-f12');if(!root||root.getAttribute('data-mdwrf12-on'))return;root.setAttribute('data-mdwrf12-on','1');
var RM=!root.classList.contains('mdwrf12-js');
var mxGen=0;
var mxZero=function(el){if(!el.hasAttribute('data-final'))el.setAttribute('data-final',el.textContent);var dec=+(el.getAttribute('data-dec')||0);el.textContent=(el.getAttribute('data-pre')||'')+(0).toFixed(dec)+(el.getAttribute('data-suf')||'');};
var mxCount=function(el,g){var to=parseFloat(el.getAttribute('data-to'));if(isNaN(to))return;var fin=el.getAttribute('data-final')||el.textContent,dec=+(el.getAttribute('data-dec')||0),pre=el.getAttribute('data-pre')||'',suf=el.getAttribute('data-suf')||'',f=new Intl.NumberFormat('en-US',{minimumFractionDigits:dec,maximumFractionDigits:dec}),t0=null,d=950;el.setAttribute('data-final',fin);var step=function(t){if(g!==mxGen)return;if(t0===null)t0=t;var p=Math.min(1,(t-t0)/d),e=1-Math.pow(1-p,3);el.textContent=pre+f.format(to*e)+suf;if(p<1)requestAnimationFrame(step);else el.textContent=fin;};requestAnimationFrame(step);};
var mxIn=function(){if(root.classList.contains('mdwrf12-in'))return;mxGen++;var g=mxGen;root.classList.add('mdwrf12-in');root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(function(el){mxCount(el,g);});};
var mxOut=function(){if(!root.classList.contains('mdwrf12-in'))return;mxGen++;root.classList.remove('mdwrf12-in');root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(mxZero);};
if(RM||!('IntersectionObserver' in window)){root.classList.add('mdwrf12-in');}else{root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(mxZero);var io=new IntersectionObserver(function(es){es.forEach(function(e){if(e.isIntersecting&&e.intersectionRatio>=.15){mxIn();}else if(!e.isIntersecting){mxOut();}});},{threshold:[0,.15]});io.observe(root);}
root.querySelectorAll('.mdwrf12-tabs').forEach(function(box){var tabs=[].slice.call(box.querySelectorAll(':scope>.mdwrf12-tablist>.mdwrf12-tab')),panels=[].slice.call(box.querySelectorAll(':scope>.mdwrf12-panel'));var pick=function(v,focus){tabs.forEach(function(t){var on=t.getAttribute('data-v')===v;t.setAttribute('aria-selected',on?'true':'false');t.tabIndex=on?0:-1;if(on&&focus)t.focus();});panels.forEach(function(p){p.hidden=p.getAttribute('data-v')!==v;});box.dispatchEvent(new CustomEvent('mdwrf12-tab',{bubbles:true}));};tabs.forEach(function(t,i){t.addEventListener('click',function(){pick(t.getAttribute('data-v'));});t.addEventListener('keydown',function(e){var k=e.key,j=k==='ArrowRight'?i+1:k==='ArrowLeft'?i-1:-1;if(j<0&&k!=='ArrowLeft')return;e.preventDefault();var n=tabs[(j+tabs.length)%tabs.length];pick(n.getAttribute('data-v'),true);});});});
var mxTip=function(host){var tip=host.querySelector(':scope>.mdwrf12-tip');if(!tip){tip=document.createElement('div');tip.className='mdwrf12-tip';tip.setAttribute('aria-hidden','true');host.appendChild(tip);}return{show:function(x,y,h){tip.innerHTML=h;tip.style.left=x+'px';tip.style.top=y+'px';tip.classList.add('mdwrf12-on');},hide:function(){tip.classList.remove('mdwrf12-on');}};};
try{
root.querySelectorAll('.mdwrf12-rg').forEach(function(c){var plot=c.querySelector('.mdwrf12-rg-plot');if(!plot||!plot.hasAttribute('tabindex'))return;
var svg=plot.querySelector('svg'),gs=[].slice.call(plot.querySelectorAll('.mdwrf12-rg-r')),labs=[].slice.call(plot.querySelectorAll('.mdwrf12-rg-l')),tks=[].slice.call(plot.querySelectorAll('.mdwrf12-rg-tk')),lens=plot.querySelector('.mdwrf12-rg-lens'),cr=plot.querySelector('.mdwrf12-rg-cr'),live=c.querySelector('.mdwrf12-rg-live');
var A=function(k){return +c.getAttribute(k);},lo=A('data-lo'),hi=A('data-hi'),top=A('data-top'),rh=A('data-rh'),HT=A('data-h'),AX=28,VB=95,gl=(c.getAttribute('data-gl')||'').split('|'),n=gs.length,step=1,cur=-1,tp=mxTip(plot);
var X=function(s){return String(s).replace(/&/g,'&amp;').replace(/</g,'&lt;').replace(/>/g,'&gt;').replace(/"/g,'&quot;');};
var nice=function(span,count){var raw=span/Math.max(1,count)||1,p=Math.pow(10,Math.floor(Math.log10(raw))),u=raw/p;return(u>=5?5:u>=2?2:1)*p;};
var R=gs.map(function(g,i){var base=top+(i+1)*rh,ln=g.querySelector('.mdwrf12-rg-ln').getAttribute('d'),nums=ln.match(/-?\d*\.?\d+/g).map(Number),y=nums[1],h=[(base*10-y)/10],k,q=g.getAttribute('data-x').split(',').map(Number);for(k=3;k<nums.length;k+=2){y+=nums[k];h.push((base*10-y)/10);}
return{g:g,base:base,h:h,q1:q[0],md:q[1],q3:q[2],m:g.getAttribute('data-m'),mh:g.getAttribute('data-mh'),r:g.getAttribute('data-r'),e:(g.getAttribute('data-e')||'').split(',').map(Number),fill:g.querySelector('.mdwrf12-rg-f').getAttribute('d'),line:ln,lab:labs[i]?labs[i].textContent:''};});
var hAt=function(r,u){if(u<=0||u>=VB)return 0;var j=Math.min(VB-1,Math.floor(u));return r.h[j]+(r.h[j+1]-r.h[j])*(u-j);};
var yv=function(r,h){return Math.round((r.base-h)*10);};
var band=function(r){var u1=r.q1,u3=r.q3,d='M'+u1+' '+r.base*10+'L'+u1+' '+yv(r,hAt(r,u1)),j;for(j=0;j<r.h.length;j++){if(j>u1&&j<u3)d+='L'+j+' '+yv(r,r.h[j]);}return d+'L'+u3+' '+yv(r,hAt(r,u3))+'L'+u3+' '+r.base*10+'Z';};
var showLens=function(i){var r=R[i];lens.setAttribute('style',r.g.getAttribute('style'));lens.innerHTML='<path class="mdwrf12-rg-lf" d="'+r.fill+'"/><path class="mdwrf12-rg-lb" d="'+band(r)+'"/><line class="mdwrf12-rg-lm" x1="'+r.md+'" x2="'+r.md+'" y1="'+r.base*10+'" y2="'+yv(r,hAt(r,r.md))+'"/><path class="mdwrf12-rg-ll" d="'+r.line+'"/>';};
var geom=function(){var a=plot.getBoundingClientRect(),b=svg.getBoundingClientRect();return{l:b.left-a.left,w:b.width};};
var clamp=function(v,a,b){return Math.min(b,Math.max(a,v));};
var tipAt=function(ax,ay,w){var t=plot.querySelector(':scope>.mdwrf12-tip');if(!t)return;t.style.transform='none';var tw=t.offsetWidth,th=t.offsetHeight,l=ax+14,y;if(l+tw>w)l=ax-14-tw;y=ay-th-14;if(y<0)y=ay+14;t.style.left=clamp(l,0,Math.max(0,w-tw))+'px';t.style.top=clamp(y,0,Math.max(0,HT-th))+'px';};
var set=function(i,v,py){var g=geom(),r,j,ax,ay,f;
if(i!==cur){cur=i;gs.forEach(function(el,k){el.classList.toggle('mdwrf12-dm',i>=0&&k!==i);el.classList.toggle('mdwrf12-lf',k===i);});labs.forEach(function(el,k){el.classList.toggle('mdwrf12-on',k===i);});if(i>=0)showLens(i);lens.classList.toggle('mdwrf12-on',i>=0);}
if(i<0){plot.removeAttribute('data-a');plot.removeAttribute('data-c');tp.hide();live.textContent='';return;}
r=R[i];f=clamp((v-lo)/(hi-lo||1),0,1);j=Math.round(f*95);plot.setAttribute('data-a','');plot.setAttribute('data-c','');cr.style.setProperty('--f',f.toFixed(4));
var note=r.e[j]+'% at or below '+gl[j];
ax=g.l+f*g.w;ay=py!=null?py:r.base-hAt(r,r.md)-6;
tp.show(ax,ay,'<div class="mdwrf12-rg-tt"><b>'+X(r.lab)+'</b><span><i>Median</i>'+X(r.m)+'</span><span><i>Middle half</i>'+X(r.mh)+'</span><span><i>Range</i>'+X(r.r)+'</span><em>'+X(note)+'</em></div>');
tipAt(ax,ay,plot.clientWidth);live.textContent=r.lab+': median '+r.m+', middle half '+r.mh+', range '+r.r+'. '+note;};
var valueAt=function(x,g){return lo+clamp((x-g.l)/(g.w||1),0,1)*(hi-lo);};
var pick=function(x,y,g){var v=valueAt(x,g),u=clamp((x-g.l)/(g.w||1),0,1)*VB,best=-1,i;for(i=0;i<n;i++){var r=R[i];if(y<=r.base+2&&y>=r.base-hAt(r,u)-4)best=i;}if(best<0)best=clamp(Math.floor((y-top)/rh),0,n-1);return{i:best,v:v};};
var clear=function(){set(-1);};
var move=function(e){var a=plot.getBoundingClientRect(),x=e.clientX-a.left,y=e.clientY-a.top,g=geom();if(x<g.l-8||y>HT-AX+4){if(e.pointerType==='mouse')clear();return;}var h=pick(x,y,g);set(h.i,h.v,y);};
plot.addEventListener('pointermove',move);plot.addEventListener('pointerdown',move);plot.addEventListener('pointerleave',function(e){if(e.pointerType==='mouse')clear();});plot.addEventListener('blur',clear);
var cv=null;var pk=function(i,v){cv=v;set(i,v,null);};
plot.addEventListener('keydown',function(e){var k=e.key,at=cur,choose=function(i){e.preventDefault();i=clamp(i,0,n-1);pk(i,cur>=0&&cv!=null?cv:R[i].md/VB*(hi-lo)+lo);};
if(k==='ArrowDown')choose(at+1);else if(k==='ArrowUp')choose(at<0?0:at-1);else if(k==='Home')choose(0);else if(k==='End')choose(n-1);
else if(k==='ArrowRight'||k==='ArrowLeft'){e.preventDefault();if(at<0){choose(0);return;}var b=cv!=null?cv:R[at].md/VB*(hi-lo)+lo;pk(at,clamp((Math.round(b/step)+(k==='ArrowRight'?1:-1))*step,lo,hi));}
else if(k==='Escape'&&at>=0){e.preventDefault();clear();}});
var fit=function(){var w=plot.clientWidth;if(!w)return;if(w<420)plot.setAttribute('data-n','');else plot.removeAttribute('data-n');var g=geom(),co=nice(hi-lo,2),m,ok=true;step=nice(hi-lo,Math.max(2,Math.floor(Math.max(40,g.w)/72)));
for(;;){var prev=-1e9;ok=true;tks.forEach(function(t){var v=+t.getAttribute('data-v'),q=v/step,on=Math.abs(q-Math.round(q))<=1e-6,s=t.firstChild;t.classList.toggle('mdwrf12-rg-off',!on);s.style.translate='';s.style.visibility='';if(!on)return;var hw=s.offsetWidth/2,x=g.l+(v-lo)/(hi-lo)*g.w,d=Math.max(0,x+hw-w)||Math.min(0,x-hw);if(d)s.style.translate='calc(-50% - '+d+'px) 0';if(x-d-hw<prev+8)ok=false;prev=x-d+hw;});

---

## Step 29: Text block 12

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

Of the 5,850 change issues, 81% were still open at the snapshot, and 87% of those open items were waiting on the Authority's project manager. Approval times for changes raised in 2020 and 2021 look shorter only because the slow ones had not finished yet.

 
### Late but under budget

 Schedule and budget can move in opposite directions. Of 134 finished NYC school construction phases:

 - **Late:** 65% ended after the date originally planned.
- **Size of the delay:** The median late finish was 100 days past plan, and 32 phases ran more than a year late.
- **Under budget:** Yet the median final estimate came in 7.8% under the award budget, and 81% of these phases finished under budget.

 Design phases looked similar, with 49% late and a median 9.9% under budget.

---

## Step 31: Text block 13

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

### New York State Thruway

 On the Thruway, 304 completed contracts took a median **327 days** from letting to completion.

 - **Spread:** A quarter took under 212 days and a quarter took more than 486.
- **By size:** Time rose with contract size, from a median of 264 days under $1 million to 673 days for the 19 contracts between $10 million and $100 million.

 That is a benchmark for a single state authority, and it measures from the letting date, not from the start of design.

 
### Victorian state projects

 Victorian state budget documents show how often finish dates move. Across 293 projects that appear in two or more budget years, **47%** had their completion date pushed back.

 - **Typical slip:** The typical slip among those was 4 quarters.
- **Long slips:** About a quarter slipped by more than 8 quarters.

 The Victorian Auditor-General's Office reviewed 110 major projects worth $149.33 billion in a report tabled in March 2026 and found that the state budget document for the capital program [does not always include meaningful information about changes to cost, scope and completion dates](https://www.audit.vic.gov.au/report/major-projects-performance-reporting-2025). That gap limits how reliable any count of slipped projects can be, ours included.

## Step 33: Text block 14

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

**What this means for project teams:** Approval time is a cost of its own.

- Track who is holding each change and for how long.
- Report schedule and budget together, because they can move in opposite directions.
- Show the original budget and date next to the status colour, or the colour can mislead.

<div class="mdwrfaq-acc"><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-item"><h3 class="mdwrfaq-acc-h"><button type="button" class="mdwrfaq-acc-btn" id="mastt-dwr-faq-faqb146" aria-expanded="false" aria-controls="mastt-dwr-faq-faqp147"><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-tw"><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-title">How often does Texas DOT excuse rain days when gauges show little rain?</span></span><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-ico" aria-hidden="true"></span></button></h3><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-panel" id="mastt-dwr-faq-faqp147" role="region" aria-labelledby="mastt-dwr-faq-faqb146"><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-in"><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-body"><p>On 47% of the 2,170 weekdays Texas DOT credited for rain, county gauges showed under a tenth of an inch on the day or the day before, and 32% were fully dry. That is a signal rather than proof, because site conditions can lag rain and gauges can miss patchy storms.</p></div></div></div></div><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-item"><h3 class="mdwrfaq-acc-h"><button type="button" class="mdwrfaq-acc-btn" id="mastt-dwr-faq-faqb148" aria-expanded="false" aria-controls="mastt-dwr-faq-faqp149"><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-tw"><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-title">Do wetter seasons cause more late penalties on Texas DOT jobs?</span></span><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-ico" aria-hidden="true"></span></button></h3><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-panel" id="mastt-dwr-faq-faqp149" role="region" aria-labelledby="mastt-dwr-faq-faqb148"><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-in"><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-body"><p>No. 12% of working-day contracts were charged late penalties in both the driest and the wettest fifth of seasons. The three middle fifths were higher, at 16% to 18%, so there is no straight-line pattern.</p></div></div></div></div><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-item"><h3 class="mdwrfaq-acc-h"><button type="button" class="mdwrfaq-acc-btn" id="mastt-dwr-faq-faqb150" aria-expanded="false" aria-controls="mastt-dwr-faq-faqp151"><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-tw"><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-title">Why does Florida DOT show 12.5% delay on one measure and 48.7% on another?</span></span><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-ico" aria-hidden="true"></span></button></h3><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-panel" id="mastt-dwr-faq-faqp151" role="region" aria-labelledby="mastt-dwr-faq-faqb150"><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-in"><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-body"><p>The 431 contracts in Florida DOT's 2025-26 workbook used 48.7% more days than planned, but 12.5% more once 32,701 weather days and 19,228 holiday days were removed. The adjustment removes 74% of the extra days.</p></div></div></div></div><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-item"><h3 class="mdwrfaq-acc-h"><button type="button" class="mdwrfaq-acc-btn" id="mastt-dwr-faq-faqb152" aria-expanded="false" aria-controls="mastt-dwr-faq-faqp153"><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-tw"><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-title">What share of active Texas DOT jobs are past their allowed days?</span></span><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-ico" aria-hidden="true"></span></button></h3><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-panel" id="mastt-dwr-faq-faqp153" role="region" aria-labelledby="mastt-dwr-faq-faqb152"><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-in"><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-body"><p>About one in six. 17.1% of active jobs in January 2017 and 16.4% in November 2024 had used more than all of their allowed days.</p></div></div></div></div><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-item"><h3 class="mdwrfaq-acc-h"><button type="button" class="mdwrfaq-acc-btn" id="mastt-dwr-faq-faqb154" aria-expanded="false" aria-controls="mastt-dwr-faq-faqp155"><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-tw"><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-title">How big are Texas DOT late penalties?</span></span><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-ico" aria-hidden="true"></span></button></h3><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-panel" id="mastt-dwr-faq-faqp155" role="region" aria-labelledby="mastt-dwr-faq-faqb154"><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-in"><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-body"><p>The median penalty was $18,000, or 0.7% of contract value, and 22% of 10,041 completed construction contracts were charged one. Total penalties of $109 million compare with $57 billion of contract awards.</p></div></div></div></div><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-item"><h3 class="mdwrfaq-acc-h"><button type="button" class="mdwrfaq-acc-btn" id="mastt-dwr-faq-faqb156" aria-expanded="false" aria-controls="mastt-dwr-faq-faqp157"><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-tw"><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-title">Which risks do project risk registers leave out?</span></span><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-ico" aria-hidden="true"></span></button></h3><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-panel" id="mastt-dwr-faq-faqp157" role="region" aria-labelledby="mastt-dwr-faq-faqb156"><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-in"><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-body"><p>Only 14% of 64 public registers list contractor insolvency, 5% treat climate change as a project risk and none mention cyber risk. By contrast 88% list community risks and 83% list approvals.</p></div></div></div></div><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-item"><h3 class="mdwrfaq-acc-h"><button type="button" class="mdwrfaq-acc-btn" id="mastt-dwr-faq-faqb158" aria-expanded="false" aria-controls="mastt-dwr-faq-faqp159"><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-tw"><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-title">How long do NYC school change orders take to approve?</span></span><span class="mdwrfaq-acc-ico" aria-hidden="true"></span></button></h3><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-panel" id="mastt-dwr-faq-faqp159" role="region" aria-labelledby="mastt-dwr-faq-faqb158"><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-in"><div class="mdwrfaq-acc-body"><p>The median was 224 days among 1,095 approved change orders in a November 2021 snapshot, and a quarter took longer than 516 days. In Queens the median was 396 days and in Brooklyn 166.</p></div></div></div></div></div>

var RM=!root.classList.contains('mdwrfaq-js');
var mxGen=0;
var mxZero=function(el){if(!el.hasAttribute('data-final'))el.setAttribute('data-final',el.textContent);var dec=+(el.getAttribute('data-dec')||0);el.textContent=(el.getAttribute('data-pre')||'')+(0).toFixed(dec)+(el.getAttribute('data-suf')||'');};
var mxCount=function(el,g){var to=parseFloat(el.getAttribute('data-to'));if(isNaN(to))return;var fin=el.getAttribute('data-final')||el.textContent,dec=+(el.getAttribute('data-dec')||0),pre=el.getAttribute('data-pre')||'',suf=el.getAttribute('data-suf')||'',f=new Intl.NumberFormat('en-US',{minimumFractionDigits:dec,maximumFractionDigits:dec}),t0=null,d=950;el.setAttribute('data-final',fin);var step=function(t){if(g!==mxGen)return;if(t0===null)t0=t;var p=Math.min(1,(t-t0)/d),e=1-Math.pow(1-p,3);el.textContent=pre+f.format(to*e)+suf;if(p<1)requestAnimationFrame(step);else el.textContent=fin;};requestAnimationFrame(step);};
var mxIn=function(){if(root.classList.contains('mdwrfaq-in'))return;mxGen++;var g=mxGen;root.classList.add('mdwrfaq-in');root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(function(el){mxCount(el,g);});};
var mxOut=function(){if(!root.classList.contains('mdwrfaq-in'))return;mxGen++;root.classList.remove('mdwrfaq-in');root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(mxZero);};
if(RM||!('IntersectionObserver' in window)){root.classList.add('mdwrfaq-in');}else{root.querySelectorAll('[data-to]').forEach(mxZero);var io=new IntersectionObserver(function(es){es.forEach(function(e){if(e.isIntersecting&&e.intersectionRatio>=.15){mxIn();}else if(!e.isIntersecting){mxOut();}});},{threshold:[0,.15]});io.observe(root);}
root.querySelectorAll('.mdwrfaq-tabs').forEach(function(box){var tabs=[].slice.call(box.querySelectorAll(':scope>.mdwrfaq-tablist>.mdwrfaq-tab')),panels=[].slice.call(box.querySelectorAll(':scope>.mdwrfaq-panel'));var pick=function(v,focus){tabs.forEach(function(t){var on=t.getAttribute('data-v')===v;t.setAttribute('aria-selected',on?'true':'false');t.tabIndex=on?0:-1;if(on&&focus)t.focus();});panels.forEach(function(p){p.hidden=p.getAttribute('data-v')!==v;});box.dispatchEvent(new CustomEvent('mdwrfaq-tab',{bubbles:true}));};tabs.forEach(function(t,i){t.addEventListener('click',function(){pick(t.getAttribute('data-v'));});t.addEventListener('keydown',function(e){var k=e.key,j=k==='ArrowRight'?i+1:k==='ArrowLeft'?i-1:-1;if(j<0&&k!=='ArrowLeft')return;e.preventDefault();var n=tabs[(j+tabs.length)%tabs.length];pick(n.getAttribute('data-v'),true);});});});

## Step 35: Text block 15

Paste into the post body (Rich Text).

## Sources and methodology

 Our own figures come from an internal Mastt study of public records. These include:

 - **Texas DOT:** More than 30,000 contract time and payment records, daily time charges matched to county rain gauge records, and monthly progress reports for two dates.
- **Florida DOT:** A state workbook of 431 contracts for 2025-26.
- **Risk registers:** 64 published project risk registers.
- **New York:** New York City school construction records covering 5,850 change issues and more than 13,000 project phases, and Thruway capital project records.
- **Victoria:** State budget documents for three budget years.

 Definitions and limits:

 - **Scope:** Texas figures describe Texas DOT work only.
- **Completed contracts:** Figures use final estimates on construction contracts unless stated.
- **Weather:** Figures match each job to county rain gauge records over its work window and compare it with the normal for the same dates.
- **Late penalty:** A charge for days used beyond allowed days, in US dollars.
- **Recent jobs:** Recent completed jobs skew toward shorter projects because long jobs are not yet final.

 How the counts were made:

 - **Risk themes:** Counts come from keyword rules applied to the latest version of each register, with matches checked by hand where stated.
- **Hospital program:** The comparison is a line by line text comparison of six 2024 reports and a text recognition read of four 2025 reports.
- **Victoria:** Figures use three machine-readable budget years.
- **Interpretation:** Findings are associations and signals, not proof of cause.

 Includes public sector information from Australian federal, state, territory and local governments, used under Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 licences.

 
### Sources

 1. Society of Construction Law (March 2017). [Second edition of the Delay and Disruption Protocol](https://scl.org.uk/resources/news/second-edition-delay-disruption-protocol)
2. International Organization for Standardization (2018). [ISO 31000:2018 Risk management, Guidelines](https://committee.iso.org/standard/65694.html)
3. Australian Securities and Investments Commission (October 2026). [ASIC Corporate Insolvency Update, Issue 41](https://www.asic.gov.au/about-asic/corporate-publications/newsletters/asic-corporate-insolvency-update/asic-corporate-insolvency-update-issue-41-october-2026)
4. Texas Department of Transportation. [Contract time, Construction Contract Administration Handbook](https://www.txdot.gov/manuals/cst/cah/prosecution_and_progress/contract_time-bceeiiih.html)
5. Florida Department of Transportation (2016-17). [Statewide inspection guide 16C, Time Extensions](https://www.fdot.gov/docs/default-source/content-docs/construction/constadm/guidelist/inspectguidelist/fy1617/GL-16C.pdf)
6. Journal of Construction Engineering and Management (Nguyen, Kneppers, García de Soto and Ibbs) (2010). [Analysis of Adverse Weather for Excusable Delays](https://scholarscommons.fgcu.edu/esploro/outputs/journalArticle/Analysis-of-Adverse-Weather-for-Excusable/99383341611206570)
7. Berger Singerman. [What you need to know about construction weather delay claims](https://www.bergersingerman.com/news-insights/what-you-need-to-know-about-construction-weather-delay-claims)
8. Holding Redlich (March 2025). [Rain, rain go away: inclement weather in construction contracts](https://holdingredlich.com/rain-rain-go-away-inclement-weather-in-construction-contracts)
9. Victorian Auditor-General's Office (March 2026). [Major Projects Performance Reporting 2025](https://www.audit.vic.gov.au/report/major-projects-performance-reporting-2025)

 **Please cite as:** Mastt, Construction Delays, Weather Days and Risk Registers: 2026 Statistics, mastt.com.
